# Production Services Configuration

## CRITICAL: Never Modify Production Configuration Directly

**All production service configurations MUST be changed through code and CI/CD workflows.**

Directly modifying Azure settings via CLI or portal will be **OVERWRITTEN** on the next deployment.

## How Configuration Flows

```
Code Change → Commit → Push → CI Workflow → Azure Settings Applied
```

| Variable | Source | Set By |
|----------|--------|--------|
| `AZURE_OPENAI_BASE_URL` | GitHub Secrets | deploy-subscription.yml |

| `EASTUS2_OPENAI_*` | GitHub Secrets | deploy-subscription.yml |
| `STRIPE_*` | GitHub Secrets | deploy-subscription.yml |

**To update Azure OpenAI credentials:**
```bash
# Get correct values from Azure
az cognitiveservices account show --name vibe-openai-1766209650 \
  --resource-group vibe-ai-rg --query properties.endpoint -o tsv

az cognitiveservices account keys list --name vibe-openai-1766209650 \
  --resource-group vibe-ai-rg --query key1 -o tsv

# Update GitHub Secrets
gh secret set AZURE_OPENAI_BASE_URL --body "https://vibe-openai-eval.openai.azure.com/"
gh secret set AZURE_OPENAI_API_KEY --body "<key-from-above>"

# Trigger redeployment
gh workflow run "Deploy Subscription Services" --field environment=both
```

AZURE_OPENAI_ENDPOINT=https://vibe-openai-eval.openai.azure.com/

```

## Verification After Deployment

```bash
# Check Docusaurus chat settings
az staticwebapp appsettings list --name vibebrowser-docs --resource-group vibebrowser \
  -o json | jq '.properties | {deployment: .AZURE_OPENAI_DEPLOYMENT, endpoint: .AZURE_OPENAI_ENDPOINT}'

# Test chat API
curl -X POST "https://docs.vibebrowser.app/api/chat" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"messages":[{"role":"user","content":"test"}]}' | jq '.content'

# Check subscription service
curl https://api.vibebrowser.app/health
```

---

## Common Mistakes to Avoid

| Mistake | Why It's Bad | Correct Approach |
|---------|--------------|------------------|
| `az staticwebapp appsettings set` directly | Overwritten on next deploy | Edit workflow yaml, commit, push |
| Changing Azure portal settings | Not tracked in git | Use terraform or workflow |
| Editing K8s secrets manually | Lost on pod restart | Update GitHub Secrets + redeploy |
| Creating separate terraform directories | Fragmented infra | Use `services/subscription/terraform/` |
| Updating Azure OpenAI keys in pod only | Lost on next deploy | Update GitHub Secrets (`gh secret set`) then trigger deploy |

---

## Rollback Procedure

1. Revert the commit: `git revert <commit-sha>`
2. Push to master
3. Wait for CI to redeploy
4. Verify service is restored

Do NOT manually fix settings - they will be overwritten again on next deploy.

---

## Troubleshooting

### Endpoint format issues
Azure OpenAI endpoints vary by resource:
- Custom domain: `https://vibe-openai-eval.openai.azure.com/` (our primary)
- Regional format: `https://eastus2.cognitiveservices.azure.com/` (AI Services)

If scripts fail, verify:
1. Endpoint format matches SDK expectations
2. Deployment name is exact (case-sensitive)
3. API version is supported

### Rate limiting
1. Check capacity in `variables.tf`
2. Increase capacity (up to Azure quota limits)
3. Run `terraform apply`

### Budget Alerts
Monthly budget: $300 USD with alerts at 50%, 80%, 100%.
Alerts sent to: `info@vibebrowser.app`

## Langfuse Integration Guide
**File:** `docs/langfuse-integration.md`
**Category:** infrastructure

# Langfuse Integration Guide

This document describes how to set up and use Langfuse for observability and debugging of the Vibe AI Browser Agent.

## What is Langfuse?

Langfuse is an open-source LLM observability and analytics platform that helps you:
- Track all LLM calls and their performance
- Debug agent execution issues
- Analyze token usage and costs
- Monitor response times and errors
- View detailed traces of multi-step agent workflows

## Architecture

The integration uses:
- **Langfuse SDK** (`langfuse`) - Core client library
- **Langfuse LangChain** (`langfuse-langchain`) - LangChain callback handler

All LLM calls made through the agent are automatically traced and sent to Langfuse.

## Deployment Options

### Option 1: Azure Deployment (Recommended)

Deploy Langfuse to your Azure account using the provided script:

```bash
# Deploy Langfuse to Azure
./scripts/deploy-langfuse-azure.sh
```

This will:
1. Create a PostgreSQL Flexible Server
2. Deploy Langfuse as an Azure Container Instance
3. Configure networking and security
4. Save credentials to `.env.langfuse`

**Cost Estimate**: ~$30-50/month for basic usage (1 CPU, 2GB RAM, 32GB storage)

### Option 2: Langfuse Cloud

Use the hosted version at https://cloud.langfuse.com:

1. Create an account at https://cloud.langfuse.com
2. Create a new project
3. Get your API keys from Settings > API Keys
4. Run `./scripts/configure-langfuse.sh`

**Cost**: Free tier available, paid plans start at $59/month

### Option 3: Self-Hosted

Deploy Langfuse using Docker:

```bash
# Clone Langfuse
git clone https://github.com/langfuse/langfuse.git
cd langfuse

# Start with Docker Compose
docker-compose up -d
```

Then run `./scripts/configure-langfuse.sh` with your self-hosted URL.

## Configuration

### Step 1: Deploy Langfuse

Choose one of the deployment options above.

### Step 2: Configure the Extension

Run the configuration script:

```bash
./scripts/configure-langfuse.sh
```

This will:
1. Prompt for your Langfuse URL and API keys
2. Update the `.env` file with Langfuse credentials
3. Save configuration to `.env.langfuse` for future reference

### Step 3: Load Environment Variables

```bash
export $( < .env )
```

### Step 4: Build and Test

```bash
# Build the extension
npm run build

# Run tests
npm test
```

## Usage

### Viewing Traces

1. Visit your Langfuse URL (e.g., http://your-instance.eastus.azurecontainer.io:3000)
2. Navigate to your project
3. Click on "Traces" to see all agent executions
4. Click on a trace to view detailed information:
   - LLM calls and responses
   - Tool executions
   - Token usage
   - Execution time
   - Error details

### Analyzing Agent Behavior

Langfuse provides several views to analyze agent behavior:

**Traces View**: See the full execution flow
- All LLM calls in chronological order
- Input/output for each step
- Execution time breakdown

**Sessions View**: Group related agent invocations
- Track multi-turn conversations
- Analyze session-level metrics

**Metrics Dashboard**: Aggregate statistics
- Total token usage
- Average response time
- Error rates
- Cost analysis

### Debugging Failed Evaluations

When an evaluation test fails, use Langfuse to:

1. Find the trace for that test run
2. View the timeline of LLM calls
3. Identify where the agent got stuck or looped
4. Check token usage to see if context window was exhausted
5. Examine error messages and stack traces

Example workflow:
```bash
# Run evaluation
npm run eval

# If it fails, check Langfuse for the trace
# Look for:
# - Repeated tool calls (doom loop)
# - High token usage (context exhaustion)
# - Long response times (timeout)
# - Error messages
```

## Managing Azure Deployment

Use the management script to control your Azure deployment:

```bash
# Check status
./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh status

# View logs
./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh logs

# Restart container
./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh restart

# Stop container (to save costs)
./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh stop

# Start container
./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh start

# Delete entire deployment
./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh delete
```

## Environment Variables

The following environment variables control Langfuse integration:

```bash
# Required for Langfuse to work
LANGFUSE_PUBLIC_KEY=pk-lf-...       # Your project public key
LANGFUSE_SECRET_KEY=sk-lf-...       # Your project secret key
LANGFUSE_BASE_URL=https://...       # Langfuse instance URL

# Optional (for Azure deployment management)
LANGFUSE_RESOURCE_GROUP=...         # Azure resource group name
LANGFUSE_CONTAINER_GROUP=...        # Azure container group name
LANGFUSE_POSTGRES_SERVER=...        # PostgreSQL server name
```

## Troubleshooting

### Langfuse not logging traces

1. Check that environment variables are set:
   ```bash
   echo $LANGFUSE_PUBLIC_KEY
   echo $LANGFUSE_SECRET_KEY
   echo $LANGFUSE_BASE_URL
   ```

2. Check browser console for Langfuse errors:
   ```
   [Langfuse] Integration enabled for LLM observability
   ```

3. Verify Langfuse is accessible:
   ```bash
   curl $LANGFUSE_BASE_URL/api/health
   ```

### Azure deployment issues

1. Check container logs:
   ```bash
   ./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh logs
   ```

2. Verify container is running:
   ```bash
   ./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh status
   ```

3. Check PostgreSQL connectivity:
   ```bash
   az postgres flexible-server show \
     --resource-group vibe-langfuse-rg \
     --name <your-postgres-server>
   ```

### High costs

If Azure costs are too high:

1. Stop the container when not in use:
   ```bash
   ./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh stop
   ```

2. Use Langfuse Cloud free tier instead

3. Reduce PostgreSQL tier:
   ```bash
   az postgres flexible-server update \
     --resource-group vibe-langfuse-rg \
     --name <your-server> \
     --sku-name Standard_B1ms
   ```

## Best Practices

1. **Use descriptive trace names**: Langfuse automatically uses the agent prompt as trace name
2. **Tag traces**: Add metadata to traces for easier filtering
3. **Monitor token usage**: Set up alerts for high token usage
4. **Regular cleanup**: Archive or delete old traces to save storage
5. **Cost monitoring**: Enable Azure Cost Management alerts

## Security

- Store `.env.langfuse` securely and never commit to git
- Use Azure Key Vault for production credentials
- Restrict PostgreSQL firewall rules to specific IPs
- Enable SSL for PostgreSQL connections
- Use Azure Private Link for container-to-database communication

## Additional Resources

- [Langfuse Documentation](https://langfuse.com/docs)
- [Langfuse GitHub](https://github.com/langfuse/langfuse)
- [Azure Container Instances Pricing](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/pricing/details/container-instances/)
- [Azure PostgreSQL Pricing](https://azure.microsoft.com/en-us/pricing/details/postgresql/)

# Langfuse Deployment Options - Comprehensive Comparison

## What Happened with the Initial Deployment

**Issues Encountered:**
1. **Region restrictions**: eastus, westus2 don't support PostgreSQL Flexible Server
2. **Provider registration**: Required manual registration of `Microsoft.DBforPostgreSQL` and `Microsoft.ContainerInstance`
3. **Script timeout**: PostgreSQL was created successfully but script timed out waiting for response
4. **Container API issues**: Missing `--os-type` parameter in Azure CLI command

**Root Cause**: Azure subscription wasn't pre-configured with all required resource providers. This is common with new subscriptions.

**What's Working Now (ALL FIXED):**
- ✅ PostgreSQL Flexible Server created and ready in centralus
- ✅ Database created
- ✅ Firewall configured
- ✅ Container Instances deployment fixed (added --os-type Linux)
- ✅ App Service deployment fixed (added WEBSITES_PORT=3000)
- ✅ Container Apps deployment script created (RECOMMENDED)

---

## Deployment Option Comparison

### 1. Azure Container Instances (ACI) ⭐ Current Approach

**What It Is:** Serverless containers without managing VMs

**Pros:**
- ✓ Simplest Azure-native option
- ✓ No cluster management
- ✓ Pay per second
- ✓ Fast startup (<1 minute)
- ✓ Good for development/staging

**Cons:**
- ✗ Limited scalability
- ✗ No auto-scaling
- ✗ Basic networking
- ✗ Less suitable for production

**Cost:** ~$35/month (1 vCPU, 2GB RAM) + $12/month (PostgreSQL)
**Total: ~$47/month**

**Best For:** Development, testing, small teams

---

### 2. Azure Container Apps ⭐⭐ RECOMMENDED

**What It Is:** Managed Kubernetes-based platform (built on AKS/KEDA)

**Pros:**
- ✓ Auto-scaling (0 to N replicas)
- ✓ Built-in ingress with SSL
- ✓ Better networking (VNet integration)
- ✓ Revision management (blue/green deployments)
- ✓ Can add PostgreSQL as managed add-on
- ✓ Production-ready
- ✓ No cluster management
- ✓ Scale to zero (cost savings)

**Cons:**
- ✗ Slightly more complex than ACI
- ✗ Requires VNet understanding

**Cost:**
- Free tier: 180,000 vCPU-seconds, 360,000 GiB-seconds/month
- After free tier: ~$50-80/month (with PostgreSQL)

**Best For:** Production, scalable applications, cost optimization

**Deployment:**
```bash
# Create Container App with PostgreSQL add-on
az containerapp env create \
  --name langfuse-env \
  --resource-group vibe-langfuse-rg \
  --location centralus

az postgres flexible-server create # (already done)

az containerapp create \
  --name langfuse \
  --resource-group vibe-langfuse-rg \
  --environment langfuse-env \
  --image ghcr.io/langfuse/langfuse:3 \
  --target-port 3000 \
  --ingress external \
  --env-vars NODE_ENV=production \
  --secrets database-url=... \
  --cpu 1 --memory 2Gi \
  --min-replicas 0 --max-replicas 3
```

---

### 3. Azure Kubernetes Service (AKS) ⭐⭐⭐ Enterprise

**What It Is:** Fully managed Kubernetes cluster

**Pros:**
- ✓ Full Kubernetes features
- ✓ Production-grade
- ✓ Advanced networking/security
- ✓ Multi-app hosting
- ✓ Helm charts support
- ✓ Complete control

**Cons:**
- ✗ Most expensive
- ✗ Requires Kubernetes expertise
- ✗ Overhead for single app
- ✗ More complex management

**Cost:**
- Control plane: Free
- Worker nodes: ~$73/month (2x Standard_B2s) minimum
- PostgreSQL: $12/month
**Total: ~$85/month minimum**

**Best For:** Large organizations, multiple applications, Kubernetes experts

**Deployment:**
```bash
az aks create \
  --resource-group vibe-langfuse-rg \
  --name langfuse-cluster \
  --node-count 2 \
  --node-vm-size Standard_B2s \
  --enable-managed-identity

kubectl apply -f langfuse-deployment.yaml
```

---

### 4. VM with k3s ⭐⭐ Cost-Effective Advanced

**What It Is:** Lightweight Kubernetes (k3s) on a single VM

**Pros:**
- ✓ Full Kubernetes capabilities
- ✓ Lower cost than AKS
- ✓ Can host multiple apps
- ✓ Complete control
- ✓ Good learning environment

**Cons:**
- ✗ Manual VM management
- ✗ Manual k3s maintenance
- ✗ Single point of failure
- ✗ Manual backups needed

**Cost:**
- VM (Standard_B2s): ~$30/month
- PostgreSQL: $12/month
**Total: ~$42/month**

**Best For:** Learning Kubernetes, cost-conscious teams, tech-savvy users

**Deployment:**
```bash
# Create VM
az vm create \
  --resource-group vibe-langfuse-rg \
  --name langfuse-vm \
  --image Ubuntu2204 \
  --size Standard_B2s \
  --admin-username azureuser \
  --generate-ssh-keys

# SSH and install k3s
ssh azureuser@<vm-ip>
curl -sfL https://get.k3s.io | sh -

# Deploy Langfuse
kubectl apply -f langfuse.yaml
```

---

### 5. Azure App Service (Containers) ⚠️ Limited

**What It Is:** PaaS for web applications

**Pros:**
- ✓ Simple deployment
- ✓ Built-in CI/CD
- ✓ Auto-scaling
- ✓ Good for web apps

**Cons:**
- ✗ Less flexible for containers
- ✗ More expensive than ACI
- ✗ Limited to HTTP/HTTPS
- ✗ Not ideal for Langfuse

**Cost:** ~$55/month (Basic tier) + $12/month (PostgreSQL)

**Best For:** Traditional web apps, not recommended for Langfuse

---

### 6. Langfuse Cloud ⭐⭐⭐ Easiest

**What It Is:** Official Langfuse SaaS

**Pros:**
- ✓ Zero infrastructure management
- ✓ 5-minute setup
- ✓ Free tier (50k observations/month)
- ✓ Automatic updates
- ✓ Professional support
- ✓ High availability

**Cons:**
- ✗ Data not in your Azure
- ✗ Less control
- ✗ Ongoing subscription cost

**Cost:**
- Free: 50k observations/month
- Hobby: $59/month (500k observations)
- Pro: $299/month (5M observations)

**Best For:** Fast setup, teams without DevOps, testing phase

**Setup:**
```bash
# 1. Go to https://cloud.langfuse.com
# 2. Create account
# 3. Create project
# 4. Get API keys
./scripts/configure-langfuse.sh
```

---

## Recommendation Matrix

| Use Case | Best Option | Why |
|----------|-------------|-----|
| **Quick Testing** | Langfuse Cloud | No infrastructure, 5 minutes |
| **Development** | Azure Container Apps | Auto-scale, cost-effective |
| **Production (Small Team)** | Azure Container Apps | Best balance of features/cost |
| **Production (Enterprise)** | AKS | Full control, multi-app support |
| **Cost-Conscious** | VM + k3s | Most cost-effective self-hosted |
| **Learning K8s** | VM + k3s | Full K8s features, low cost |
| **Current ACI** | Migrate to Container Apps | Better features, similar complexity |

---

## My Recommendation: Azure Container Apps

**Why Container Apps?**

1. **Best Balance**: Production features without cluster complexity
2. **Cost Optimization**: Scale to zero when not in use
3. **Future-Proof**: Built on Kubernetes, can migrate to AKS later
4. **Better than ACI**: Auto-scaling, better networking, SSL
5. **Easier than AKS**: No cluster management, no node maintenance

**Migration Path:**
```
Current: ACI (working PostgreSQL)
    ↓
Short term: Fix ACI deployment (1 hour)
    ↓
Better: Migrate to Container Apps (2 hours)
    ↓
Future: Migrate to AKS if needed (when team grows)
```

## Azure OpenAI Terraform Module
**File:** `services/subscription/terraform/README.md`
**Category:** deployment

# Azure OpenAI Terraform Module

Manages Azure OpenAI resources and model deployments for Vibe Browser using infrastructure-as-code.

## Overview

This Terraform module replaces the bash script approach to Azure OpenAI deployment with:
- **State management**: Tracks what's deployed
- **Idempotent**: Safe to run multiple times
- **Declarative**: Define desired state, Terraform handles the rest
- **Version controlled**: Infrastructure as code in Git

## Prerequisites

- Azure CLI installed and authenticated (`az login`)
- Terraform >= 1.0 installed
- Sufficient Azure permissions to create storage accounts and cognitive services

## Initial Setup (One-time)

### Step 1: Create Remote State Storage

The Terraform state is stored in Azure Storage for team collaboration and backup.

```bash
# Create resource group for Terraform state
az group create --name vibe-terraform-state --location eastus

# Create storage account (must be globally unique - adjust name if needed)
az storage account create \
  --name vibetfstate \
  --resource-group vibe-terraform-state \
  --location eastus \
  --sku Standard_LRS \
  --kind StorageV2

# Create container for state files
az storage container create \
  --name tfstate \
  --account-name vibetfstate

# (Optional) Enable blob versioning for state recovery
az storage account blob-service-properties update \
  --account-name vibetfstate \
  --enable-versioning true
```

**State Storage Details:**
- Location: Azure Blob Storage (free tier available)
- Encryption: At-rest (default Azure encryption)
- Access: Restricted to authenticated Azure users
- Locking: Prevents concurrent modifications (when backend supports)

### Step 2: Initialize Terraform

```bash
cd services/subscription/terraform/

# Download provider and initialize
terraform init
```

This will:
1. Download the Azure provider
2. Create local `.terraform/` directory
3. Configure backend connection to Azure Storage
4. Validate Terraform files

## Importing Existing Resources

After initialization, import the existing Azure OpenAI resource and deployments:

```bash
# Get your subscription ID
SUBSCRIPTION_ID=$(az account show --query id -o tsv)

# Import the Azure OpenAI resource
terraform import azurerm_cognitive_account.openai \
  /subscriptions/$SUBSCRIPTION_ID/resourceGroups/vibe-ai-rg/providers/Microsoft.CognitiveServices/accounts/vibe-openai-1766209650

# Import existing model deployments
terraform import azurerm_cognitive_deployment.gpt_5_mini \
  /subscriptions/$SUBSCRIPTION_ID/resourceGroups/vibe-ai-rg/providers/Microsoft.CognitiveServices/accounts/vibe-openai-1766209650/deployments/gpt-5-mini

terraform import azurerm_cognitive_deployment.gpt_5 \
  /subscriptions/$SUBSCRIPTION_ID/resourceGroups/vibe-ai-rg/providers/Microsoft.CognitiveServices/accounts/vibe-openai-1766209650/deployments/gpt-5

terraform import azurerm_cognitive_deployment.gpt_5_nano \
  /subscriptions/$SUBSCRIPTION_ID/resourceGroups/vibe-ai-rg/providers/Microsoft.CognitiveServices/accounts/vibe-openai-1766209650/deployments/gpt-5-nano
```

### Step 3: Verify Import

After importing, verify the state:

```bash
# List imported resources
terraform state list

# Expected output:
# azurerm_cognitive_account.openai
# azurerm_cognitive_deployment.gpt_5_mini
# azurerm_cognitive_deployment.gpt_5
# azurerm_cognitive_deployment.gpt_5_nano
# data.azurerm_resource_group.vibe

# Plan should show no changes (state matches reality)
terraform plan

# Expected: "No changes. Infrastructure is up-to-date."
```

## Daily Operations

### View Current Infrastructure

```bash
# Show all resources managed by Terraform
terraform state list

# Show detailed resource info
terraform state show azurerm_cognitive_account.openai

# Get output values (endpoint, resource name, etc)
terraform output
terraform output -json   # JSON format for scripts
```

### Plan Changes

```bash
# Preview what will change (without applying)
terraform plan

# Save plan to file for review
terraform plan -out=tfplan

# Review the plan
terraform show tfplan
```

### Apply Changes

```bash
# Apply changes (will prompt for confirmation)
terraform apply

# Apply saved plan (non-interactive)
terraform apply tfplan

# Auto-approve (use with caution)
terraform apply -auto-approve
```

### Add New Model Deployment

Example: Adding a new model deployment:

```bash
# 1. Edit main.tf and add:
# resource "azurerm_cognitive_deployment" "new_model" {
#   ...
# }

# 2. Plan the change
terraform plan

# 3. Apply
terraform apply
```

### Get API Endpoint and Key

```bash
# Get endpoint URL (for AZURE_OPENAI_BASE_URL)
terraform output -raw openai_endpoint

# Get API key (requires Azure CLI - not stored in Terraform)
az cognitiveservices account keys list \
  --name vibe-openai-1766209650 \
  --resource-group vibe-ai-rg \
  --query "key1" -o tsv
```

## Integration with deploy-all.sh

Update the `deploy_azure_openai()` function in deploy-all.sh:

```bash
deploy_azure_openai() {
    echo "Deploying Azure OpenAI via Terraform..."

    # Navigate to Terraform directory
    cd "$SCRIPT_DIR/terraform" || exit 1

    # Initialize (safe if already initialized)
    terraform init || { echo "Terraform init failed"; exit 1; }

    # Apply Terraform configuration
    terraform apply -auto-approve || { echo "Terraform apply failed"; exit 1; }

    # Export outputs for LiteLLM configuration
    export AZURE_OPENAI_BASE_URL=$(terraform output -raw openai_endpoint)
    export AZURE_OPENAI_API_KEY=$(az cognitiveservices account keys list \
        --name vibe-openai-1766209650 \
        --resource-group vibe-ai-rg \
        --query "key1" -o tsv)

    echo -e "${GREEN}Azure OpenAI configured via Terraform${NC}"
    echo "  Endpoint: $AZURE_OPENAI_BASE_URL"
    echo "  Models: $(terraform output -json model_deployments)"

    # Return to subscription directory
    cd "$SCRIPT_DIR" || exit 1
}
```

## CRITICAL: Updating GitHub Secrets After Terraform Changes

**Terraform manages Azure resources, but GitHub Secrets are the source of truth for CI/CD deployments.**

After running `terraform apply`, you MUST update GitHub Secrets for the changes to take effect in production:

```bash
# Get values from Terraform/Azure
ENDPOINT=$(terraform output -raw openai_endpoint)
API_KEY=$(az cognitiveservices account keys list \
  --name vibe-openai-1766209650 \
  --resource-group vibe-ai-rg \
  --query "key1" -o tsv)

# Update GitHub Secrets (this is what deploy-subscription.yml uses)
gh secret set AZURE_OPENAI_BASE_URL --body "$ENDPOINT"
gh secret set AZURE_OPENAI_API_KEY --body "$API_KEY"

# Trigger redeployment to apply new secrets
gh workflow run "Deploy Subscription Services" --field environment=both
```

**Why this matters:**
- The `deploy-subscription.yml` workflow reads from GitHub Secrets, not Terraform state
- If you only run `terraform apply` without updating GitHub Secrets, production will use stale credentials
- Updating K8s secrets directly in the pod will be overwritten on the next deployment

## State Management & Troubleshooting

### View Remote State

```bash
# Download and view remote state
terraform state pull | jq .

# List all resources in state
terraform state list

# Show resource details
terraform state show 'azurerm_cognitive_account.openai'
```

### State Locking

If Terraform appears stuck:

```bash
# List locks
terraform force-unlock <lock-id>

# (Rarely needed - only if Terraform process crashes)
```

### State Recovery

If something goes wrong:

```bash
# Backup current state
terraform state pull > backup.tfstate

# Remove resource from state (without deleting in Azure)
terraform state rm azurerm_cognitive_deployment.gpt_5

# Re-import resource
terraform import azurerm_cognitive_deployment.gpt_5 ...
```

## Rollback Plan

If Terraform migration causes issues:

### Option 1: Revert State (Recommended)

```bash
# Restore from backup
terraform state push backup.tfstate

# Verify
terraform plan
```

### Option 2: Continue with Bash

```bash
# Remove Terraform state
rm -rf .terraform terraform.tfstate*

# Use original deploy-all.sh (still works - resources already exist)
cd ..
./deploy-all.sh
```

## Cost & Capacity

### Azure OpenAI SKU Pricing

| SKU | Cost/Month | TPM | Recommended |
|-----|-----------|-----|---|
| S0 | ~$4.00 | 90K | Testing, dev |
| S1 | ~$50.00 | 500K | Production |

Current config uses **S0 (Standard)** at ~$4/month with 90K TPM.

To upgrade:

```hcl
# In variables.tf, change default:
# default = "S1"

terraform plan    # Shows upgrade cost/time
terraform apply   # Applies upgrade
```

### Model Deployment Capacity

Each deployment has 10 capacity units. To increase:

```hcl
# In main.tf, change capacity in azurerm_cognitive_deployment blocks:
sku {
  name     = "Standard"
  capacity = 20    # Increase from 10 to 20
}

terraform apply
```

## Monitoring & Logs

### View Resource Metrics

```bash
# Get resource details
az cognitiveservices account show \
  --name vibe-openai-1766209650 \
  --resource-group vibe-ai-rg

# View API usage
az monitor metrics list \
  --resource-id $(terraform output -raw openai_id) \
  --metric "Processed Characters"
```

### Enable Diagnostic Logging

Add to main.tf for production:

```hcl
resource "azurerm_monitor_diagnostic_setting" "openai_logs" {
  name               = "openai-logs"
  target_resource_id = azurerm_cognitive_account.openai.id
  # Configure log destinations...
}
```

## Security Best Practices

1. **State File Security**:
   - State stored in Azure Storage (encrypted at rest)
   - Access controlled by Azure RBAC
   - Enable versioning for recovery

2. **Secret Management**:
   - API keys retrieved via Azure CLI (not in Terraform state)
   - Store keys in Azure Key Vault for production
   - Rotate keys regularly

3. **Resource Protection**:
   - `prevent_destroy = true` on OpenAI resource prevents accidental deletion
   - State locking prevents concurrent modifications

## Architecture Decisions

### Why Terraform?

- **State Management**: Knows what's deployed, prevents duplication
- **Plan/Apply Workflow**: Preview changes before applying
- **Version Control**: Infrastructure tracked in Git
- **Team Collaboration**: Shared state in Azure Storage
- **Idempotent**: Safe to run multiple times

### What's NOT in Terraform

Left in bash scripts for now:
- VM infrastructure (can add later)
- Docker services
- Container Apps deployment
- Cloudflare DNS

Can be migrated incrementally.

## Useful Commands Reference

```bash
# Planning
terraform plan                  # Show changes
terraform plan -out=tf.plan    # Save plan

# Applying
terraform apply                # Apply changes
terraform apply tf.plan        # Apply saved plan
terraform apply -auto-approve  # Skip confirmation (use carefully)

# State Management
terraform state list           # List resources
terraform state show <resource> # Show resource details
terraform state rm <resource>  # Remove from state
terraform state pull           # Download state
terraform state push           # Upload state

# Debugging
terraform validate             # Check syntax
terraform fmt                  # Format code
terraform console              # Interactive shell
terraform graph                # Show resource dependencies

# Cleanup (if needed)
terraform destroy              # Delete all resources (careful!)
terraform destroy -auto-approve # Skip confirmation
```

## Support & Debugging

### Common Issues

**Issue**: "terraform init" fails with backend error
```bash
# Solution: Verify Azure Storage exists
az storage account show --name vibetfstate --resource-group vibe-terraform-state
```

**Issue**: Import command fails
```bash
# Solution: Use correct resource ID format
az resource show --name vibe-openai-1766209650 --resource-group vibe-ai-rg
```

**Issue**: State conflicts between users
```bash
# Solution: Remote state with locking prevents this
# Wait for other user's operation to complete, or:
terraform force-unlock <lock-id>
```

## Subscription Service Deployment
**File:** `services/subscription/docs/deployment.md`
**Category:** services

# Subscription Service Deployment

Deployed as a docker on azure vm:  azureuser@4.246.58.241

## Overview

The Vibe Browser subscription system consists of:
- **LiteLLM Proxy** - LLM gateway with virtual key authentication (gpt-5-mini, gpt-5)
- **Stripe Service** - Subscription management and Stripe integration
- **User Portal** - Customer-facing usage dashboard

All services run on Azure VM with nginx reverse proxy and are accessible via:
- API: https://api.vibebrowser.app
- Portal: https://portal.vibebrowser.app

## Prerequisites

1. **Environment Variables** in `.env`:
   ```bash
   # Required
   LITELLM_MASTER_KEY=sk-...
   OPENAI_API_KEY=sk-proj-...
   STRIPE_SECRET_KEY=sk_test_...
   STRIPE_PRO_PRICE_ID=price_...
   STRIPE_MAX_PRICE_ID=price_...
   SUPABASE_URL=https://xxx.supabase.co
   SUPABASE_ACCESS_TOKEN=...

   # Optional
   STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET=whsec_...
   SUPABASE_JWT_SECRET=...
   CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN=...
   ```

2. **Azure CLI** authenticated (`az login`)

## Quick Deploy

```bash
# Deploy everything (recommended)
./deploy-all.sh

# Deploy specific components
./deploy-all.sh openai    # Deploy Azure OpenAI only
./deploy-all.sh portal    # Deploy user portal only
./deploy-all.sh litellm   # Deploy/update LiteLLM only
./deploy-all.sh supabase  # Configure Supabase only
./deploy-all.sh test      # Run tests only
```

## Verify Deployment

```bash
# Test LiteLLM health
curl https://api.vibebrowser.app/health

# Test subscription endpoint
curl https://api.vibebrowser.app/ap./test-user

# Test usage endpoint
curl https://api.vibebrowser.app/api/usage/test-user

# Test checkout (should return Stripe URL)
curl -X POST https://api.vibebrowser.app/api/checkout \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"user_id":"test","email":"test@example.com","plan":"pro"}'
```

## Services

| Service | Port | Endpoint |
|---------|------|----------|
| LiteLLM | 4000 | https://api.vibebrowser.app/ |
| Stripe Service | 3002 | https://api.vibebrowser.app/api/* |
| User Portal | 3001 | https://portal.vibebrowser.app |
| nginx | 80 | Reverse proxy |

## API Endpoints

### Stripe Service

| Endpoint | Method | Description |
|----------|--------|-------------|
| `/api/user/provision` | POST | Create user and return API key |
| `/ap./:user_id` | GET | Get subscription tier |
| `/api/usage/:user_id` | GET | Get usage/spend data |
| `/api/checkout` | POST | Create Stripe checkout session |
| `/api/portal` | POST | Create Stripe portal session |
| `/stripe/webhook` | POST | Stripe webhook handler |

### LiteLLM

| Endpoint | Method | Description |
|----------|--------|-------------|
| `/chat/completions` | POST | OpenAI-compatible chat API |
| `/health` | GET | Health check (requires key) |

## Stripe Configuration

### Products (Test Mode)

| Product | Price ID | Amount |
|---------|----------|--------|
| Vibe API Pro | `price_1SgLPu2EVgVHETA6JGyJGwE8` | $25/month |
| Vibe API Max | `price_1Sg8XD2EVgVHETA6wewWwbvq` | $99/month |

### Budgets

| Tier | Budget | Duration |
|------|--------|----------|
| Free | $1 | 24 hours |
| Pro | $50 | 30 days |

## Updating User Portal

To update the user portal (auth.html, static files):

### Option 1: Full Redeploy (Recommended)
```bash
cd subscription
./deploy-all.sh portal
```

### Option 2: Quick Update via SSH
```bash
# SSH to the VM
ssh azureuser@4.246.58.241

# Navigate to backend directory
cd /opt/vibe-backend

# Rebuild and restart user-portal container
docker-compose build user-portal
docker-compose up -d user-portal

# Verify
docker-compose ps user-portal
curl http://localhost:3001/auth.html | head -20
```

### Option 3: Copy files directly
```bash
# From local machine, copy updated files
scp services/subscription/user-portal/public/auth.html azureuser@4.246.58.241:/opt/vibe-backend/user-portal/public/

# SSH and restart
ssh azureuser@4.246.58.241 "cd /opt/vibe-backend && docker-compose restart user-portal"
```

## Testing

### E2E Test with Portal Auth
```bash
# Test against production portal
node tests/vibe-e2e.test.js

# Test against local portal (localhost:3001)
node tests/vibe-e2e.test.js --dev
```

## Troubleshooting

### LiteLLM workers crashing
- Check `docker logs litellm-proxy`
- Verify OPENAI_API_KEY is set
- Ensure litellm-config.yaml is valid YAML

### Stripe webhooks not received
- Configure webhook URL in Stripe Dashboard
- Set STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET in .env
- Check `/stripe/webhook` endpoint accessibility

### Usage endpoint returns "fetch failed"
- LiteLLM may still be starting
- Wait 30 seconds and retry
- Check Docker network connectivity

    └── systemDesign.md    # Architecture docs

## Vibe Browser Services Architecture
**File:** `services/design.md`
**Category:** infrastructure

# Vibe Browser Services Architecture

## Infrastructure Overview

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                              CLOUDFLARE (DNS + CDN)                          │
│                          Zone: vibebrowser.app                               │
│                                                                              │
│   vibebrowser.app ──────────► Azure Static Web Apps (Extension Website)     │
│   docs.vibebrowser.app ─────► Azure Static Web Apps (Docusaurus)            │
│   api.vibebrowser.app ──────► k3s Cluster (4.246.58.241)                    │

## Subscription Tiers

| Tier | Price | Budget | Models |
|------|-------|--------|--------|
| Free | $0/mo | $1/day | gpt-5-mini, gpt-oss-120b, gpt-4.1 |
| Pro | $25/mo | $25/mo | + gpt-5.1, grok-4-fast, deepseek-v3.2 |
| Max | $99/mo | $99/mo | + gpt-5.2, grok-4, grok-4-fast-reasoning, deepseek-r1 |

Model access: User-level restrictions in LiteLLM. API keys inherit from `user.models`.

## Stripe Configuration

**CRITICAL: Price IDs must be in sync across:**
1. Stripe Dashboard (live/test mode)
2. K8s ConfigMap (`vibe-config`)
3. K8s Secret (`STRIPE_SECRET_KEY`)

**Production Price IDs (LIVE):**
```
STRIPE_FREE_PRICE_ID=price_1Sllr4CGJfPPv7TjHBZ0TCem
STRIPE_PRO_PRICE_ID=price_1SllrgCGJfPPv7Tj4WPurlEo  
STRIPE_MAX_PRICE_ID=price_1SllsMCGJfPPv7TjJonNwly9
```

**Verify sync:**
```bash
kubectl get configmap -n vibe vibe-config -o jsonpath='{.data}' | jq .
kubectl exec -n vibe deploy/stripe-service -- printenv STRIPE_SECRET_KEY | cut -c1-10
# Should show: sk_live_51
```

**CRITICAL: Updating Azure OpenAI Secrets**

After any Azure OpenAI changes (new models, rotated keys, etc.), update GitHub Secrets:

```bash
# Get values from Azure
az cognitiveservices account show --name vibe-openai-1766209650 \
  --resource-group vibe-ai-rg --query properties.endpoint -o tsv
az cognitiveservices account keys list --name vibe-openai-1766209650 \
  --resource-group vibe-ai-rg --query key1 -o tsv

# Update GitHub Secrets
gh secret set AZURE_OPENAI_BASE_URL --body "<endpoint>"
gh secret set AZURE_OPENAI_API_KEY --body "<key>"

# Redeploy
gh workflow run "Deploy Subscription Services" --field environment=both
```

## Cloudflare DNS Management

**Zone ID:** `9f10b94c3422f57d10cc11c8539c0e6b`

**CRITICAL: SSL/TLS mode must be "Full" or "Strict" - NEVER set to "Flexible" (causes redirect loop)**

### Add DNS Record

```bash
# Load token from .env
export CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN=$(grep CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN .env | cut -d'=' -f2 | tr -d "'" | tr -d '"')
ZONE_ID="9f10b94c3422f57d10cc11c8539c0e6b"

# Add CNAME record (proxied through Cloudflare)
curl -s -X POST "https://api.cloudflare.com/client/v4/zones/$ZONE_ID/dns_records" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  --data '{
    "type": "CNAME",
    "name": "subdomain",
    "content": "target.example.com",
    "ttl": 1,
    "proxied": true
  }' | jq '.success'

# Add A record (for k3s cluster)
curl -s -X POST "https://api.cloudflare.com/client/v4/zones/$ZONE_ID/dns_records" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  --data '{
    "type": "A",
    "name": "subdomain",
    "content": "4.246.58.241",
    "ttl": 1,
    "proxied": true
  }' | jq '.success'
```

### List DNS Records

```bash
curl -s "https://api.cloudflare.com/client/v4/zones/$ZONE_ID/dns_records" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN" | jq '.result[] | {name, type, content}'
```

### Delete DNS Record

```bash
RECORD_ID="record-id-here"
curl -s -X DELETE "https://api.cloudflare.com/client/v4/zones/$ZONE_ID/dns_records/$RECORD_ID" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN" | jq '.success'
```

### Fix SSL Mode (if redirect loop occurs)

```bash
curl -X PATCH "https://api.cloudflare.com/client/v4/zones/$ZONE_ID/settings/ssl" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer $CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN" \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -d '{"value": "full"}'
```

### Azure Custom Domain Setup

When pointing Cloudflare to Azure App Service, you need to:

1. Add CNAME record in Cloudflare pointing to `*.azurewebsites.net`
2. Add TXT record for Azure domain verification:
   ```bash
   # Get verification ID from Azure error message, then:
   curl -s -X POST "https://api.cloudflare.com/client/v4/zones/$ZONE_ID/dns_records" \
     -H "Authorization: Bearer $CLOUDFLARE_API_TOKEN" \
     -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
     --data '{
       "type": "TXT",
       "name": "asuid.subdomain",
       "content": "VERIFICATION_ID_FROM_AZURE",
       "ttl": 1
     }'
   ```
3. Add custom domain in Azure:
   ```bash
   az webapp config hostname add \
     --webapp-name APP_NAME \
     --resource-group RESOURCE_GROUP \
     --hostname subdomain.vibebrowser.app
   ```

## Subscription Service Design
**File:** `services/subscription/design.md`
**Category:** services

# Subscription Service Design

## Components

| Component | Port | Description |
|-----------|------|-------------|
| LiteLLM Proxy | 4000 | OpenAI-compatible API gateway with per-user budgets |
| Stripe Service | 3002 | Subscription management and webhook handling |
| User Portal | 3001 | OAuth login flow via Supabase |

## Architecture

```
┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
│                         USER FLOW                                        │
│                                                                          │
│  Extension → portal.vibebrowser.app → Supabase OAuth → API Key          │
│                                                                          │
│  API Call → api.vibebrowser.app/v1/* → LiteLLM → Azure OpenAI           │
│                                                                          │
│  Upgrade → Stripe Checkout → Webhook → Update user.models in LiteLLM    │
└─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
```

## Model Access Control

User-level restrictions (not key-level):
- `user.models` array in LiteLLM defines allowed models
- API keys inherit from user (no key-level models)
- Stripe webhooks update `user.models` on tier change
- Existing keys automatically respect new tier

## Database Schema

### Supabase (PostgreSQL)

```sql
auth.users (id UUID, email TEXT, created_at TIMESTAMPTZ)

public.profiles (
  id UUID PRIMARY KEY REFERENCES auth.users(id),
  subscription_tier TEXT DEFAULT 'free',      -- free, pro, max
  stripe_customer_id TEXT
)
```

### LiteLLM (PostgreSQL)

```sql
litellm.users (
  user_id TEXT PRIMARY KEY,           -- Supabase UUID
  max_budget DECIMAL,
  budget_duration TEXT,               -- '24h' or '30d'
  models TEXT[],                      -- Allowed models (user-level)
  metadata JSONB                      -- {tier: 'free'|'pro'|'max'}
)

litellm.keys (
  token TEXT PRIMARY KEY,
  user_id TEXT REFERENCES users,
  models TEXT[],                      -- NULL = inherit from user
  max_budget DECIMAL
)
```

### Stripe

```
Customers: metadata.user_id = supabase UUID
Subscriptions: metadata = {user_id, plan, tier}
```

## Tier Configuration

Location: `stripe-service/server.js` BUDGETS constant

```javascript
const BUDGETS = {
  free: { max_budget: 1.0, budget_duration: '24h', models: ['gpt-5-mini', 'gpt-oss-120b', 'gpt-4.1'] },
  pro:  { max_budget: 25.0, budget_duration: '30d', models: [..., 'gpt-5.1', 'grok-4-fast', 'deepseek-v3.2'] },
  max:  { max_budget: 99.0, budget_duration: '30d', models: [..., 'gpt-5.2', 'grok-4', 'deepseek-r1'] },
};
```

Keep `scripts/manage.ts` BUDGETS in sync.

## Kubernetes Deployment

### Directory Structure

```
services/subscription/k8s/
├── base/                    # Base manifests
│   ├── kustomization.yaml
│   ├── litellm.yaml
│   ├── stripe-service.yaml
│   └── user-portal.yaml
├── overlays/
│   ├── dev/                 # Dev environment
│   │   ├── kustomization.yaml
│   │   ├── ingress.yaml
│   │   └── .env.secrets.dev
│   └── prod/                # Prod environment
│       ├── kustomization.yaml   # ConfigMap with Stripe price IDs
│       ├── ingress.yaml
│       └── .env.secrets.prod
└── .env.secrets.template    # Template for secrets
```

### Kustomize Overlays

**Base** (`k8s/base/`): Common deployments, services
**Dev** (`k8s/overlays/dev/`): vibe-dev namespace, TEST Stripe, api-dev domain
**Prod** (`k8s/overlays/prod/`): vibe namespace, LIVE Stripe, api domain

### Deploy Script

```bash
# Get cluster access first
./services/k3s/setup-k3s-access.ts

# Deploy
./services/subscription/deploy.ts deploy      # Uses Kustomize
./services/subscription/deploy.ts status      # Check pods
./services/subscription/deploy.ts logs        # View logs
./services/subscription/deploy.ts restart     # Restart deployments
```

### Manual kubectl

```bash
# Dev
kubectl apply -k services/subscription/k8s/overlays/dev

# Prod
kubectl apply -k services/subscription/k8s/overlays/prod

# Check status
kubectl get pods -n vibe
kubectl get pods -n vibe-dev
```

### Secrets Management

Secrets are managed via Kustomize secretGenerator:

```yaml
# k8s/overlays/prod/kustomization.yaml
secretGenerator:
  - name: vibe-secrets
    envs:
      - .env.secrets.prod
```

Create secrets file from template:
```bash
cp k8s/.env.secrets.template k8s/overlays/prod/.env.secrets.prod
# Edit with real values
```

Required secrets:
- `LITELLM_MASTER_KEY` - Admin access to LiteLLM
- `STRIPE_SECRET_KEY` - Stripe API key (live or test)
- `STRIPE_WEBHOOK_SECRET` - Webhook signing
- `DATABASE_URL` - PostgreSQL connection
- `SUPABASE_JWT_SECRET` - JWT verification
- `ADMIN_API_KEY` - Admin dashboard access

### ConfigMap (Environment-Specific)

```yaml
# k8s/overlays/prod/kustomization.yaml
configMapGenerator:
  - name: vibe-config
    literals:
      - STRIPE_FREE_PRICE_ID=price_xxx
      - STRIPE_PRO_PRICE_ID=price_xxx
      - STRIPE_MAX_PRICE_ID=price_xxx
```

## CI/CD Deployment

### GitHub Actions

Push to `master` with changes in `services/subscription/**` triggers:
```
validate → build-user-portal → deploy-dev → deploy-prod
```

### Manual Trigger

```bash
# Via GitHub CLI
gh workflow run deploy-subscription.yml -f environment=prod
gh workflow run deploy-subscription.yml -f environment=dev

# Via empty commit
git commit --allow-empty -m "trigger deploy" && git push
```

## User Provisioning Flow

```
1. User signs in via portal.vibebrowser.app (Supabase OAuth)
2. Supabase creates auth.users record
3. Trigger creates public.profiles record
4. Stripe Service creates:
   - Stripe Customer (if needed)
   - LiteLLM User with tier-appropriate models
   - LiteLLM API Key
5. Key returned to user
```

## File Reference

| File | Purpose |
|------|---------|
| `stripe-service/server.js` | BUDGETS, webhooks, admin API |
| `scripts/manage.ts` | CLI for granting subscriptions |
| `litellm-config.yaml` | Model routing, pricing |
| `k8s/overlays/*/kustomization.yaml` | Environment config |

## Vibe AI Browser Extension
**File:** `README.md`
**Category:** monitoring

# Vibe AI Browser Extension

Chromium Agentic Web Browser component extension implementing world-class AI agent architecture for web automation with AI-first browsing experience.


## 🧠 AI Agent Architecture

Our AI agent implements a **LangGraph State Machine with Reflection Pattern**, advancing beyond traditional ReAct architectures to provide more robust planning, execution, and self-correction capabilities.

### Research Foundation

Based on state-of-the-art approaches:
- **[LangGraph Framework](https://langchain-ai.github.io/langgraph/)** - State machine patterns for agent workflows
- **[Reflection Pattern in AI Agents](https://arxiv.org/abs/2303.11366)** - Self-critique and improvement loops
- **[Plan-and-Execute Agents](https://blog.langchain.dev/planning-agents/)** - Milestone-based task decomposition

### Core Architecture: Plan-Execute-Reflect Loop

**Documentation**: See [Extension Documentation](docs/extension.md) for detailed architecture.

### Available Architectures

The agent supports two execution architectures:

#### 1. Plan-Execute-Reflect (Default)
- Full reflection pattern with tool and milestone analysis
- Best for complex, multi-step tasks requiring verification
- Includes retry logic with specific suggestions
- Higher overhead but more robust

#### 2. Plan-Execute
- Simpler architecture based on [LangGraph's plan-and-execute pattern](https://blog.langchain.dev/planning-agents/)
- Replanning after each step without deep reflection
- Lower overhead, suitable for straightforward tasks
- Configure with: `config.architecture = 'plan-execute'`

**Reference**: Based on [LangGraph's plan-and-execute pattern](https://blog.langchain.dev/planning-agents/)

#### 3. CLI Mode (No Browser - 30 seconds)
```bash
export $(< .env)
node cli.js run "find cheapest flights to Paris"
```

### Requirements
- Chrome/Chromium 88+ (for Manifest V3)
- Node.js 18+
- API Keys in `.env` file:
```bash
OPENAI_API_KEY=sk-...

## 🔍 Observability with Langfuse

Langfuse integration provides comprehensive observability for debugging agent execution issues, analyzing token usage, and monitoring performance.

**Quick Start:**
```bash
# Deploy Langfuse to Azure
./scripts/deploy-langfuse-azure.sh

# Configure credentials
./scripts/configure-langfuse.sh

# Load environment and build
export $( < .env )
npm run build
```

**Features:**
- Trace all LLM calls and tool executions
- Analyze token usage and costs
- Debug failed evaluations with timeline analysis
- Monitor response times and error rates

**Documentation**: See [Langfuse Integration Guide](docs/langfuse-integration.md)

**Management:**
```bash
./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh status   # Check deployment status
./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh logs     # View logs
./scripts/manage-langfuse-azure.sh restart  # Restart container
```
# Optional: Sentry error tracking
SENTRY_DSN=https://your-key@sentry.io/project-id
SENTRY_ENVIRONMENT=development
GEMINI_API_KEY=...
```

## 🚀 Quick Start

### Three Ways to Run Vibe

#### 1. Regular Chrome Extension (Easiest - 1 minute)
```bash
# Build and launch
npm run build:extension
cp .env dist/extension/.env
chrome --load-extension=$(pwd)/dist/extension
```

**Production Build:**
```bash
# Full production deployment with Sentry source map upload
./scripts/deploy-production.sh                    # Build + package
./scripts/deploy-production.sh --no-publish       # Build only
./scripts/deploy-production.sh --publish          # Build + publish to Chrome Web Store

# Beta testing: Push version tag → auto-publishes to trusted testers
git tag v1.0.0 && git push origin v1.0.0

# Outputs:
#   dist/VibeBrowserExtension-v{VERSION}-webstore.zip  (Chrome Web Store)
#   dist/VibeBrowserExtension-v{VERSION}.crx           (Direct install)
```

**Configuration:**
```bash
# .env file (required for production)
SENTRY_DSN=https://xxx@sentry.io/zzz          # Error tracking
SENTRY_ORG=your-org
SENTRY_PROJECT=your-project
SENTRY_AUTH_TOKEN=your-token
```

**Features:**
- Code obfuscation with import/export preservation
- Source maps uploaded to Sentry before deletion
- Chrome Web Store publishing automation
- CRX package creation for enterprise deployment

#### 2. Component Extension (Built into Chromium - 8-10 hours)
```bash
ninja -C out/Debug chrome
out/Debug/Chromium.app/Contents/MacOS/Chromium
```

#### 3. CLI Mode (No Browser - 30 seconds)
```bash
export $(< .env)
node cli.js run "find cheapest flights to Paris"
```

### Testing
Once launched, test these features:
1. **New Tab**: Opens Vibe chat interface
2. **Omnibox**: Type `vibe what is 2+2` → Should respond "4"
3. **Flight Booking**: Type `vibe BOOK A FLIGHT SFO JFK DEC 1 ONE WAY`

| Mode | Setup Time | Use Case |
|------|------------|----------|
| Extension | 1 minute | Testing/Development |
| Component | 8-10 hours | Production browser |
| CLI | 30 seconds | Automation/Scripts |

## 🔍 Search Tools Setup (Recommended)

The AI agent uses search tools for web searches. We recommend **Tavily** for best results:

### Get Your Free Tavily API Key (1 minute)

1. **Visit Tavily**: Go to [https://tavily.com](https://tavily.com)
2. **Sign Up**: Click "Get API Key" (no credit card required)
3. **Copy Your Key**: From the dashboard, copy your API key

### Configure Your Environment

```bash
# Add to your .env file
echo "TAVILY_API_KEY='tvly-YOUR_API_KEY_HERE'" >> .env

# Or export directly
export TAVILY_API_KEY='tvly-YOUR_API_KEY_HERE'

# Load environment variables before running
export $( < .env )
```

### Why Tavily?

- **Free Tier**: 1000 searches/month at no cost
- **AI-Optimized**: Returns clean, structured results perfect for LLMs
- **No Bot Detection**: Works reliably unlike browser-based searches
- **Includes AI Answers**: Provides summarized answers when available

**Fallback**: If no API key is set, the agent automatically uses free DuckDuckGo search (unlimited but lower quality).

## Build & Test

```bash
# Install dependencies
npm install

# Build component extension (integrates into Chromium build)
ninja -C out/Debug chrome

# Run tests
npm test                     # Main test suite
npm run test:extension:puppeteer # Puppeteer-based extension tests

# Test browser integration
ninja -C out/Debug chrome
out/Debug/Chromium.app/Contents/MacOS/Chromium
```

## Chromium Integration

The extension is built into Chromium as a **component extension** through:

1. **Resource Packaging** (`component_extension_resources.grd`):
   ```xml
   <!-- Vibe Extension - Essential Files Only -->
   <include name="IDR_VIBE_MANIFEST" file="${root_gen_dir}/chrome/browser/resources/vibe/manifest.json" .../>
   <include name="IDR_VIBE_CHAT_HTML" file="${root_gen_dir}/chrome/browser/resources/vibe/chat.html" .../>
   <include name="IDR_VIBE_BACKGROUND_JS" file="${root_gen_dir}/chrome/browser/resources/vibe/background.js" .../>
   ```

2. **Auto-loading** (`component_loader.cc`):
   ```cpp
   Add(IDR_VIBE_MANIFEST, base::FilePath(FILE_PATH_LITERAL("vibe")));
   ```

Files are bundled into `component_extension_resources.pak` and loaded automatically on browser startup.

## 📬 Message Communication Architecture

The extension uses a sophisticated event-driven architecture with two layers of messaging:

### Communication Flow Diagram

```mermaid
graph TB
    subgraph "Browser Tab"
        CP[ChatPage.tsx<br/>React UI]
    end

    subgraph "Service Worker"
        BG[background.js<br/>Message Broker]
    end

    subgraph "Agent System"
        AA[ai_agent.js<br/>Orchestrator]
        RG[ReactGraph.js<br/>LangGraph Agent]
        ES[EventStore<br/>Event Buffer]

        AA --> RG
        RG --> ES
        ES --> AA
    end

    subgraph "Tools"
        NAV[NavigationTools]
        INT[InteractionTools]
        MEM[MemoryTools]
        SRCH[SearchTools]
    end

    %% Transport Layer Messages (thick lines)
    CP -.->|"USER_QUESTION<br/>{query, conversationId}"| BG
    BG -.->|"AI_UPDATE<br/>{events[], conversationId}"| CP

    BG ==>|"AI_AGENT_REQUEST<br/>{action, query}"| AA
    AA ==>|"updates via<br/>EventStore"| BG

    %% Semantic Events (thin lines)
    RG -->|"thinking event"| ES
    RG -->|"tool event"| ES
    RG -->|"error event"| ES

    %% Tool Execution
    RG -->|"execute"| NAV
    RG -->|"execute"| INT
    RG -->|"execute"| MEM
    RG -->|"execute"| SRCH

    NAV -->|"result"| RG
    INT -->|"result"| RG
    MEM -->|"result"| RG
    SRCH -->|"result"| RG

    %% Event Collection
    AA -->|"user event"| ES
    AA -->|"assistant event"| ES
    AA -->|"AGENT_STOPPED event"| ES

    style BG fill:#f9f,stroke:#333,stroke-width:4px
    style AA fill:#bbf,stroke:#333,stroke-width:2px
    style RG fill:#bfb,stroke:#333,stroke-width:2px
    style ES fill:#fbf,stroke:#333,stroke-width:2px
    style CP fill:#ff9,stroke:#333,stroke-width:2px
```

### Message Types

#### Transport Layer Messages
These are Chrome runtime messages that flow between components:

| Message Type | Direction | Purpose |
|--------------|-----------|---------|
| `USER_QUESTION` | ChatPage → background.js | User submits a query |
| `AI_UPDATE` | background.js → ChatPage | Batched events for UI update |
| `AI_AGENT_REQUEST` | background.js → ai_agent.js | Start/stop agent execution |
| `OPEN_SIDE_PANEL` | Omnibox → background.js | Open side panel from omnibox |
| `GET_CONVERSATION` | ChatPage → background.js | Retrieve conversation history |
| `SAVE_CONVERSATION` | background.js → Storage | Persist conversation |

#### Semantic Event Types
These are agent state events that flow through the EventStore:

| Event Type | Source | Description |
|------------|--------|-------------|
| `user` | ai_agent.js | User's original query |
| `thinking` | ReactGraph.js | LLM's reasoning/thoughts |
| `tool` | ReactGraph.js | Tool execution details |
| `assistant` | ai_agent.js | Final agent response |
| `error` | Any component | Error information |
| `AGENT_STOPPED` | ai_agent.js | Agent state/completion |
| `plan` | ReactGraph.js | Execution plan (internal) |
| `memory_update` | MemoryTools | Memory operations |
| `page_content_captured` | NavigationTools | Page content extraction |

#### Ephemeral vs Persistent Messages

The agent distinguishes between **ephemeral** (display-only) and **persistent** (stored) messages:

| Message Type | `app_type` | Persisted | Purpose |
|--------------|------------|-----------|---------|
| Thinking | `thinking` | No | Shows "is thinking" during LLM calls |
| Reflecting | `reflecting` | No | Shows reflection phase |
| Compressing | `compressing` | No | Shows context compression |
| Stopped | `stopped` | No | Immediate feedback on stop |
| Answer | `answer` | Yes | Final response to user |
| Thought | `thought` | Yes | Agent reasoning (tool calls) |
| Error | `error` | Yes | Error messages |

**Implementation** (`ReactGraph.ts:emitSystemEvent`):
```
state.allMessages = [user, ai, tool, ...]     // Persisted to storage
                         |
emitSystemEvent('thinking') creates ephemeral message
                         |
AI_UPDATE.messages = [...allMessages, ephemeral]  // Sent for display
                         |
Next AI_UPDATE replaces entire messages[] -> old ephemeral gone
```

Ephemeral messages are appended to `messages[]` in the AI_UPDATE event but NOT added to `state.allMessages`. This ensures:
1. UI shows real-time status (thinking, reflecting)
2. Status automatically disappears when next update arrives
3. Session history remains clean without transient states

### Tool Workflow

```mermaid
sequenceDiagram
    participant User
    participant ChatPage
    participant Background
    participant Agent
    participant ReactGraph
    participant Tool

    User->>ChatPage: Enter query
    ChatPage->>Background: USER_QUESTION
    Background->>Agent: AI_AGENT_REQUEST
    Agent->>ReactGraph: Execute agent

    loop Think-Act-Observe Cycle
        ReactGraph->>ReactGraph: Think (LLM reasoning)
        ReactGraph-->>Agent: thinking event
        ReactGraph->>Tool: Execute tool
        Tool-->>ReactGraph: Tool result
        ReactGraph-->>Agent: tool event
        ReactGraph->>ReactGraph: Observe result
    end

    ReactGraph-->>Agent: Final result
    Agent-->>Background: assistant event
    Background-->>ChatPage: AI_UPDATE
    ChatPage-->>User: Display response
```

### Event Store Pattern

The EventStore acts as a buffer between the agent system and UI:
- Collects events from ReactGraph and ai_agent
- Batches events for efficient message passing
- Preserves event ordering with timestamps
- Enables replay and debugging of agent execution

The extension includes a **mock LLM server** for testing without API keys or costs. The mock server simulates OpenAI API responses for the complete agent workflow.

./scripts/build-test-extension.sh real --upload

# Test with mock server (no API key required)
./scripts/build-test-extension.sh mock
```

### Mock Server Features

- **No API Keys Required**: Test without OpenAI/Gemini credentials
- **Fast & Reliable**: Instant responses, no rate limits
- **Full Agent Simulation**: Supports think, execute, and reflect nodes
- **Tool Calling Support**: Simulates browser navigation and interactions
- **CI/CD Ready**: Perfect for automated testing pipelines

### Test Output

Tests generate:
- **Screenshots**: Visual validation at each step in `.test/[timestamp]/screenshots/`
- **Logs**: Detailed execution logs in `.test/[timestamp]/logs/`
- **Reports**: Can be uploaded to GitHub PRs with `--upload` flag

## Home page, workflow start
  ┌─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │                        USER QUERY FROM HOMEPAGE                          │
  └─────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                      │
                                      ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 1. HomePage.tsx                                                          │
  │    - User types query (non-URL)                                          │
  │    - handleSubmit() creates sessionId                                    │
  │    - Sends: chrome.runtime.sendMessage({                                 │
  │        type: 'UI:MESSAGE',                                               │
  │        content: query,                                                   │
  │        sessionId: sessionId,                                             │
  │        config: { mode: 'act' }                                           │
  │      })                                                                  │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                      │
                                      ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 2. background.js - newMessageHandler()                                   │
  │    - Gets active tab ID                                                  │
  │    - Opens sidepanel: chrome.sidePanel.open({ tabId })                   │
  │    - Creates/gets agent for sessionId                                    │
  │    - Sets callback on AskUserQuestionTool instance:                      │
  │      askUserTool.setCallback(async (questionData) => {                   │
  │        messages = agent.workflow.state.messages;                         │
  │        chrome.runtime.sendMessage({                                      │
  │          type: 'AI_UPDATE',                                              │
  │          sessionId,                                                      │
  │          messages,                                                       │
  │          userQuestion: questionData                                      │
  │        });                                                               │
  │      })                                                                  │
  │    - Starts agent: agent.invoke(content, { sessionId, config })          │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                      │
                                      ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 3. VibeLangchainAgent (ai_agent.js)                                      │
  │    - Creates ReactAgent with tools                                       │
  │    - Executes workflow                                                   │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                      │
                                      ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 4. ReactAgent (ReactGraph.js)                                            │
  │    - Processes query                                                     │
  │    - Calls tools (e.g., ask_user_question)                               │
  │    - Sends AI_UPDATE after each step:                                    │
  │      this.onEvent(EVENT_TYPES.AI_UPDATE, {...state})                     │
  │      ↓ includes state.messages[]                                         │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                      │
                           ┌──────────┴──────────┐
                           │                     │
                           ▼                     ▼
  ┌────────────────────────────────┐  ┌────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 5a. Normal AI_UPDATE           │  │ 5b. ask_user_question Tool     │
  │     background.js sends:       │  │     Calls this.onUserQuestion  │
  │     {                          │  │     callback which sends:      │
  │       type: 'AI_UPDATE',       │  │     {                          │
  │       sessionId,               │  │       type: 'AI_UPDATE',       │
  │       messages: [...]          │  │       sessionId,               │
  │     }                          │  │       messages: [...],         │
  │                                │  │       userQuestion: {          │
  │                                │  │         requestId,             │
  │                                │  │         question,              │
  │                                │  │         context,               │
  │                                │  │         suggestions            │
  │                                │  │       }                        │
  │                                │  │     }                          │
  └────────────────────────────────┘  └────────────────────────────────┘
                           │                     │
                           └──────────┬──────────┘
                                      ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 6. ChatPage.tsx - handleMessage()                                        │
  │    - Receives AI_UPDATE                                                  │
  │    - If sessionId doesn't exist → create new session                     │
  │    - Process messages[] from AI_UPDATE                                   │
  │    - If event.userQuestion exists:                                       │
  │      * Set isWaitingForUserInput = true                                  │
  │      * Append question message with suggestions                          │
  │    - Displays: User query → AI responses → Question (if any)             │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘
                                      │
                                      ▼
  ┌──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┐
  │ 7. User responds to question                                             │
  │    - ChatPage sends: chrome.runtime.sendMessage({                        │
  │        type: 'USER_QUESTION_RESPONSE',                                   │
  │        requestId,                                                        │
  │        response: userAnswer                                              │
  │      })                                                                  │
  │    - AskUserQuestionTool receives response, resolves Promise             │
  │    - Agent continues execution                                           │
  └──────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────────┘

## Google Workspace MCP Server
**File:** `services/subscription/k8s/README-MCP.md`
**Category:** kubernetes

# Google Workspace MCP Server

MCP (Model Context Protocol) server providing Gmail, Calendar, and Drive access via OAuth tokens from the Chrome extension.

## Endpoint

- **URL:** `http://mcp.api.vibebrowser.app/mcp`
- **Protocol:** MCP 2024-11-05 over HTTP with SSE

## Commands to Verify State

### Check Pod Status

```bash
# List MCP pods
kubectl get pods -n vibe-mcp

# Get detailed pod info
kubectl describe pod -n vibe-mcp -l app=google-workspace-mcp
```

### Check Logs

```bash
# Tail logs
kubectl logs -n vibe-mcp -l app=google-workspace-mcp --tail=50

# Follow logs
kubectl logs -n vibe-mcp -l app=google-workspace-mcp -f
```

### Test MCP Endpoint

```bash
# Test initialize (no auth required)
curl -s http://mcp.api.vibebrowser.app/mcp -X POST \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -H "Accept: application/json, text/event-stream" \
  -d '{"jsonrpc":"2.0","method":"initialize","params":{"protocolVersion":"2024-11-05","capabilities":{},"clientInfo":{"name":"test","version":"1.0"}},"id":1}'

# Test tools/list (no auth required)
curl -s http://mcp.api.vibebrowser.app/mcp -X POST \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -H "Accept: application/json, text/event-stream" \
  -d '{"jsonrpc":"2.0","method":"tools/list","params":{},"id":2}'

# Test tool call (requires Bearer token with Google OAuth access token)
curl -s http://mcp.api.vibebrowser.app/mcp -X POST \
  -H "Content-Type: application/json" \
  -H "Accept: application/json, text/event-stream" \
  -H "Authorization: Bearer YOUR_GOOGLE_OAUTH_TOKEN" \
  -d '{"jsonrpc":"2.0","method":"tools/call","params":{"name":"health_check","arguments":{}},"id":3}'
```

### Service & Ingress

```bash
# Check service
kubectl get svc -n vibe-mcp

# Check ingress
kubectl get ingress -n vibe-mcp

# Check endpoints
kubectl get endpoints -n vibe-mcp
```

### Restart Deployment

```bash
kubectl rollout restart deployment/google-workspace-mcp -n vibe-mcp
kubectl rollout status deployment/google-workspace-mcp -n vibe-mcp
```

### Deploy Changes

```bash
# Manual deployment
kubectl apply -f services/subscription/k8s/google-workspace-mcp.yaml

# Via GitHub Actions (on merge to master)
# Workflow: .github/workflows/deploy-mcp.yml
# Automatically deploys when google-workspace-mcp.yaml changes
```

## GitHub Actions Deployment

The MCP server is automatically deployed via GitHub Actions on merge to master.

**Workflow:** `.github/workflows/deploy-mcp.yml`

**Secrets Required:**
- `K3S_KUBECONFIG` - Base64 encoded kubeconfig for k3s cluster
- `GOOGLE_OAUTH_TOKEN` - Google OAuth access token for testing

**Manual Trigger:**
```bash
gh workflow run deploy-mcp.yml
```

**View Workflow Runs:**
```bash
gh run list --workflow=deploy-mcp.yml
```

## Available Tools

| Tool | Description | Auth Required |
|------|-------------|---------------|
| `health_check` | Verify server and auth status | Yes |
| `list_emails` | List Gmail messages | Yes |
| `send_email` | Send email via Gmail | Yes |
| `list_calendar_events` | List Google Calendar events | Yes |
| `list_drive_files` | List Google Drive files | Yes |

## Authentication Flow

1. Extension obtains Google OAuth token via Chrome Identity API
2. Token stored in extension storage linked to MCP server URL
3. MCP client reads token and adds `Authorization: Bearer <token>` header
4. Server validates token on tool calls (not on initialize/tools/list)

## Troubleshooting

### Pod not starting

```bash
# Check events
kubectl get events -n vibe-mcp --sort-by='.lastTimestamp'

# Check resource limits
kubectl top pod -n vibe-mcp
```

### 401 Unauthorized on tool calls

- Verify Google OAuth token is valid and not expired
- Check token has required scopes (gmail, calendar, drive)

### Connection refused

```bash
# Verify pod is running
kubectl get pods -n vibe-mcp

# Check service selector matches pod labels
kubectl get pods -n vibe-mcp --show-labels
kubectl get svc -n vibe-mcp -o yaml | grep selector -A5
```

## How Claude Opus 4.5 Cost Me $12,000 in 3 Days
**File:** `docs/blogpost-ptu-fuckup.md`
**Category:** deployment

# How Claude Opus 4.5 Cost Me $12,000 in 3 Days

*A cautionary tale about blindly trusting AI-generated infrastructure code*

---

## TL;DR

I asked Claude Opus 4.5 to write Terraform for deploying Azure OpenAI models. It deployed `gpt-5.1-codex` with PTU (Provisioned Throughput) instead of pay-per-token pricing. The model was never used. Cost: **$12,000 in a few days**.

---

## The Setup

I was migrating our Azure OpenAI deployments from bash scripts to Terraform. The goal was simple:
- Better state management
- Reproducible infrastructure
- Version-controlled deployments

I asked Claude Opus 4.5 to help write the Terraform configuration. It generated clean, well-documented code:

```terraform
# Deployment: gpt-5.1-codex on Foundry (westus3)
# Code-specialized model
resource "azurerm_cognitive_deployment" "gpt_5_1_codex" {
  name                 = "gpt-5-1-codex"
  cognitive_account_id = azurerm_cognitive_account.foundry_openai.id

  model {
    format  = "OpenAI"
    name    = "gpt-5.1-codex"
    version = "2025-11-13"
  }

  scale {
    type     = "DataZoneProvisionedManaged"  # <-- THE $12,000 MISTAKE
    capacity = 100
  }
}
```

The code looked professional. Had comments. Followed Terraform best practices. I reviewed it, ran `terraform apply`, and moved on.

---

## The Discovery

A week later, I was reviewing Azure costs for an unrelated reason. Something was wrong:

```
Resource: vibe-foundry-openai
Region: westus3
This month's cost: $12,847.32
```

What the fuck.

I checked the deployment. Zero requests. Zero tokens processed. The model wasn't even configured in our LiteLLM proxy - it was never integrated into the application.

**$12,000 for a model that was never called once.**

---

## What Went Wrong

### The Problem: PTU vs Pay-Per-Token

Azure OpenAI has two pricing models that look similar in Terraform but cost wildly different amounts:

| Scale Type | Billing | Monthly Cost (100 capacity) |
|------------|---------|----------------------------|
| `GlobalStandard` | Pay-per-token | $0 if unused |
| `DataZoneStandard` | Pay-per-token | $0 if unused |
| `DataZoneProvisionedManaged` | **Fixed reservation** | **~$12,000** |

PTU (Provisioned Throughput Units) is designed for high-volume production workloads that need guaranteed capacity. You pay ~$120/PTU/month **regardless of whether you use it**.

Claude chose `DataZoneProvisionedManaged` without any justification. The commit message simply said:

> "Use DataZoneProvisionedManaged SKU for codex model"

No explanation of why. No cost warning. No mention that this would charge $12K/month even with zero usage.

### Why I Didn't Catch It

1. **The code looked correct** - Proper Terraform syntax, good comments, followed conventions
2. **I didn't know the pricing difference** - `DataZoneProvisionedManaged` sounds like a reasonable Azure SKU name
3. **No cost estimation in terraform plan** - Terraform doesn't show Azure costs
4. **The model deployed successfully** - No errors, no warnings
5. **I trusted the AI** - It had been helpful all day, why would this be wrong?

---

## The Fix

Took 30 seconds:

```bash
# Delete the deployment
az cognitiveservices account deployment delete \
  --name vibe-foundry-openai \
  --resource-group vibe-ai-rg \
  --deployment-name gpt-5-1-codex

# Delete the empty account
az cognitiveservices account delete \
  --name vibe-foundry-openai \
  --resource-group vibe-ai-rg
```

Then removed the Terraform resources so they wouldn't be recreated.

---

## What Should Have Been Done

The correct Terraform for a new deployment:

```terraform
resource "azurerm_cognitive_deployment" "gpt_5_1_codex" {
  name                 = "gpt-5-1-codex"
  cognitive_account_id = azurerm_cognitive_account.foundry_openai.id

  model {
    format  = "OpenAI"
    name    = "gpt-5.1-codex"
    version = "2025-11-13"
  }

  scale {
    type     = "GlobalStandard"  # Pay-per-token, $0 if unused
    capacity = 100
  }
}
```

One line difference. $12,000 difference.

---

## Lessons Learned

### 1. AI Doesn't Understand Cost Implications

Claude Opus 4.5 is excellent at writing syntactically correct code. It has no concept of:
- How much things cost
- Whether a resource will actually be used
- The difference between "works" and "works economically"

### 2. Always Verify Cloud Pricing Models

Before deploying any cloud resource, understand:
- What triggers billing?
- Is it pay-per-use or reserved capacity?
- What happens if usage is zero?

For Azure OpenAI specifically:
- `GlobalStandard` = pay-per-token (safe for new deployments)
- `DataZoneStandard` = pay-per-token (safe for new deployments)  
- `*Provisioned*` = **fixed monthly cost** (only for high-volume production)

### 3. Set Up Billing Alerts

I should have had alerts for:
- Daily spend > $100
- Any new resource costing > $50/day
- Unexpected resource creation

Azure Cost Management can do this. I just hadn't configured it.

### 4. Review AI-Generated Infra Code Line by Line

When Claude generates application code and it has a bug, you get an error message. When Claude generates infrastructure code and it has a cost bug, you get an invoice.

Treat AI-generated Terraform/CloudFormation/Bicep with extra scrutiny:
- Look up every SKU/tier/capacity setting
- Understand the billing model for each resource
- Check if the resource is actually needed

### 5. Verify Resources Are Actually Used

Before deploying any model/service:
- Is it referenced in application config?
- Will traffic actually route to it?
- What happens if it's never called?

The `gpt-5.1-codex` model was never added to our LiteLLM config. It couldn't receive requests even if users tried.

---

## The Silver Lining

This expensive mistake led to a full audit of our Azure resources. We found:
- Another orphaned development resource (`vibe-dev`)
- Duplicate OpenAI accounts in different resource groups
- Unused deployments from previous experiments

Total savings after cleanup: ~$200/month in recurring waste, plus preventing future PTU accidents.

---

## Updated AGENTS.md

I added this to our AI coding guidelines:

```markdown
### Never Use PTU (Provisioned Throughput) Without Active Usage
**Mistake**: Deployed `gpt-5.1-codex` with `DataZoneProvisionedManaged` (PTU).
**Problem**: PTU charges ~$120/PTU/month regardless of usage. Zero requests = $12K wasted.
**Solution**: 
1. Always use `GlobalStandard` or `DataZoneStandard` (pay-per-token)
2. Verify model is referenced in application config before deploying
3. Review Azure billing weekly for unexpected charges

// BAD - PTU reservation ($12k/month fixed cost)
scale {
  type     = "DataZoneProvisionedManaged"
  capacity = 100
}

// GOOD - Pay-per-token (only pay for what you use)
scale {
  type     = "GlobalStandard"
  capacity = 100
}
```

---

## Conclusion

AI coding assistants are incredibly powerful. Claude Opus 4.5 has saved me hundreds of hours. But it optimizes for "code that works," not "code that's economical."

For infrastructure code specifically:
- The blast radius of mistakes is your cloud bill
- There are no unit tests for "costs too much"
- AI doesn't know or care about pricing

Trust but verify. Especially the billing model.

---

*The $12,000 model that was never used once. An expensive reminder that AI-generated code still needs human review - especially when it touches your wallet.*


---
